The Signature Force Sequences Database

march to a million: in progress

Archive records: 24 / 1,000,000 target · 24 records (populated from the live archive index)

The force sequences database holds one ordered sequence of force applications per record: its steps in order, a verdict per step, and a verdict for the whole sequence, whether every step holds, holds only under a stated condition, or fails, runs away, or collapses. Each record states the physics basis in net force per step, equilibrium conditions, and thresholds where established, and carries a deterministic force-sequence demo that recomputes every step's verdict from fixed inputs. The Signature edition carries original Signature sequence designs for the Signature network, each labeled as a design study, never presented as an established public fact. Distinction, stated plainly: the live network holds force fields and force-and-motion basics, and the sibling pair holds force combinations, but no database of ordered force sequences with a collapse-or-not verdict per step and per sequence; this pair is that missing verdict archive, one sequence per record, ordered simple to compounded to complex. No record here carries weapons design data of any kind, and no sequence is given any weapons application. Twenty-four full records, ordered simple to compounded to complex, each written in human-readable sentences and paragraphs, each carrying its ordered steps with the forces at each step, its sequence rule, its per-step and whole-sequence collapse verdicts, its physics basis, a worked example, a history, a science proof, a math proof, a formal statement, a scientific equation, a proof sketch, a runnable demo program whose output is reproduced on the page, and a named specialist AI. Every demo adds the member force vectors at each step, compares the net force with the force that step's motion requires, runs the whole sequence twice, and matches the two SHA-256 hashes; no demo is a laboratory measurement, and no record carries weapons design data.

Specialist AI: Signature JAH Force Sequence Designer

Signature JAH Force Sequence Designer answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the ladder from simple to compounded to complex. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as an established public fact.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Open the page script to use window.ForceSequenceSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, a subject, a problem of at least 400 characters, a method, ordered sequence steps with forces, magnitudes and directions at each step, a sequence rule, a collapse verdict, a physics basis, a worked example, a history, an evidence label with its reason, a verification path, a science proof, a math proof, a formal statement, a scientific equation, a proof sketch, a named source, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run and whose verdict matches the stated collapse verdict.

Records

Elevator Ride: Motor Pull, Cruise, Brake, Stop Rank 1 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature elevator test rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature elevator test rig: a 1200 kilogram test car riding upward, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.2.

This Signature design study proposes a test rig for teaching elevator control: a 1200 kilogram car on a short hoist tower, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.2. The car weighs 11,772 newtons. To accelerate upward the rig's motor must pull with 13,212 newtons, so the net force is 1440 newtons up. At cruising speed the motor pulls with 11,772 newtons, the net force is zero, and the car rises steadily. To slow down the motor pulls with 10,332 newtons, the net force is 1440 newtons down, and the car loses speed. At the floor the motor pulls with 11,772 newtons again and the car stands still. The rig adds a load cell on the cable so students can watch the pull change at each step.

History

The Signature rig design starts from the same history as the public record, Otis's 1853 safety brake and the electric traction elevator, and turns it into a teaching machine: a hoist tower, a car, and a load cell. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 as the first Signature force-sequence design, deliberately the simplest possible ride, so that the four-step control pattern could be proven on hardware before any more complicated sequence was built on it.

Method

Build the hoist tower and hang the 1200 kilogram test car from the cable.; Read the load cell and compute the weight from mass times gravity.; Set the acceleration and compute the motor pull needed for the first step.; Check each step's net force against the net force that step's motion requires.; Record the four load-cell readings as the rig's baseline calibration..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Accelerate up — forces:
    • Motor pull: 13212 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 11772 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 1440 N; actual net force Fx = -1.35348e-12 N, Fy = 1440 N; difference 1.35348e-12 N (tolerance 6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Cruise — forces:
    • Motor pull: 11772 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 11772 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.44165e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.44165e-12 N (tolerance 6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Brake — forces:
    • Motor pull: 10332 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 11772 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -1440 N; actual net force Fx = -1.52983e-12 N, Fy = -1440 N; difference 1.52983e-12 N (tolerance 6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Stop — forces:
    • Motor pull: 11772 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 11772 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.44165e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.44165e-12 N (tolerance 6 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: accelerate, cruise, brake, stop. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: motor pull 13,212 newtons in the accelerate step. A motor that cannot supply it cannot start the test car upward.

Physics basis

Step 1, accelerate up: motor pull 13,212 newtons up against gravity 11,772 newtons down gives a net force of 1440 newtons up. Step 2, cruise: pull 11,772 newtons against gravity 11,772 newtons gives zero net force at steady speed. Step 3, brake: pull 10,332 newtons against gravity 11,772 newtons gives 1440 newtons down, which slows the car. Step 4, stop: pull 11,772 newtons against gravity 11,772 newtons gives zero net force and the car stands still.

Worked example

A student runs the Signature rig through a full ride. The load cell reads 13,212 newtons as the car accelerates upward, drops to 11,772 newtons as the car cruises, falls to 10,332 newtons as the car brakes, and returns to 11,772 newtons as the car stops at the floor. The four readings, printed side by side, show the whole control pattern in one run.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature elevator test rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 1200 times 9.81 is 11,772, and 13,212 minus 11,772 is 1440, matching the accelerate step, while 10,332 minus 11,772 is minus 1440, matching the brake step. The cruise and stop steps are exact balances, 11,772 against 11,772, so their net force is zero.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the rig teaches the same lesson as the public record: the motor lifts by pulling a little harder than gravity while the car speeds up, exactly as hard as gravity while it cruises, and a little less hard while it slows down. The load cell makes the lesson visible, because the pull it reads is the number the design is about. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each of the four steps in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Accelerate up': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.35348e-12 N and Fy = 1440 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 1440 N; the difference is 1.35348e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.44165e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.44165e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Brake': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.52983e-12 N and Fy = -1440 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -1440 N; the difference is 1.52983e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.44165e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.44165e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Motor pull = m * g + m * a; accelerate: 11772 + 1440 = 13,212 N; cruise: 11,772 N; brake: 11772 - 1440 = 10,332 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

In plain words, the rig teaches the same lesson as the public record: the motor lifts by pulling a little harder than gravity while the car speeds up, exactly as hard as gravity while it cruises, and a little less hard while it slows down. The load cell makes the lesson visible, because the pull it reads is the number the design is about. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each of the four steps in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Proof sketch

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Accelerate up': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.35348e-12 N and Fy = 1440 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 1440 N; the difference is 1.35348e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.44165e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.44165e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Brake': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.52983e-12 N and Fy = -1440 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -1440 N; the difference is 1.52983e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.44165e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.44165e-12 N against a tolerance of 6 N, so the step HOLDS. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Elevator Ride: Motor Pull, Cruise, Brake, Stop'
steps = [('Accelerate up', [('Motor pull', 13212.0, 90), ('Gravity', 11772.0, 270)], [0.0, 1440.0], 6.0), ('Cruise', [('Motor pull', 11772.0, 90), ('Gravity', 11772.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 6.0), ('Brake', [('Motor pull', 10332.0, 90), ('Gravity', 11772.0, 270)], [0.0, -1440.0], 6.0), ('Stop', [('Motor pull', 11772.0, 90), ('Gravity', 11772.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 6.0)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Car Start, Cruise, and Braking Stop Rank 2 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature instrumented test-car design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature test car: a 900 kilogram electric test vehicle on a flat track, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9.

This Signature design study proposes a test vehicle for measuring drive and braking sequences: a 900 kilogram electric car on a flat track, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9. The car weighs 8829 newtons. To start, the motor drives forward with 2700 newtons against 180 newtons of rolling resistance, so the net force is 2520 newtons forward. At cruising speed the drive is 180 newtons, exactly matching the resistance, so the net force is zero. To stop, the brakes supply 4500 newtons backward plus 180 newtons of resistance, so the net force is 4680 newtons backward. At rest every horizontal force is zero. The design adds wheel torque sensors so each step's drive and braking force is measured, not assumed.

History

The Signature test-car design starts from the same history as the public record, the first automobiles and the drum, disc, and anti-lock brakes that followed, and turns it into an instrumented vehicle: a track car with torque sensors on every wheel. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a measured start-cruise-brake sequence, with the numbers recorded by the car itself.

Method

Build the 900 kilogram test car and fit torque sensors to the wheels.; Measure the drive force and the rolling resistance at each step.; Add the horizontal forces at each step to get the net force.; Compare each step's net force with the net force that step's motion requires.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Start — forces:
    • Drive force: 2700 N at 0 degrees
    • Rolling resistance: 180 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 8829 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 8829 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 2520 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 2520 N, Fy = 2.20436e-14 N; difference 9.09762e-13 N (tolerance 4.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Cruise — forces:
    • Drive force: 180 N at 0 degrees
    • Rolling resistance: 180 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 8829 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 8829 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.08124e-12 N, Fy = 2.20436e-14 N; difference 1.08147e-12 N (tolerance 4.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Brake — forces:
    • Braking force: 4500 N at 180 degrees
    • Rolling resistance: 180 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 8829 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 8829 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -4680 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -4680 N, Fy = 5.73135e-13 N; difference 1.07502e-12 N (tolerance 4.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Stop — forces:
    • Normal force: 8829 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 8829 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.08124e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.08124e-12 N (tolerance 4.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: start, cruise, brake, stop. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: braking force 4500 newtons in the brake step. Weaker brakes stretch the stopping distance; the sequence needs the full value.

Physics basis

Step 1, start: drive 2700 newtons forward against rolling resistance 180 newtons backward gives a net force of 2520 newtons forward. Step 2, cruise: drive 180 newtons against resistance 180 newtons gives zero net force at steady speed. Step 3, brake: braking force 4500 newtons plus resistance 180 newtons backward gives 4680 newtons backward, which slows the car. Step 4, stop: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the car stays still.

Worked example

The Signature test car pulls away from the start line on its track. The torque sensors read 2700 newtons of drive, the car accelerates, and on the straight the drive settles to 180 newtons, matching resistance, while the car cruises. At the end of the track the brakes supply 4500 newtons, the car slows, and it stops with the sensors reading zero horizontal force.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature instrumented test-car design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 2700 minus 180 is 2520, matching the start step, and 4500 plus 180 is 4680, matching the brake step. The cruise step is an exact balance, 180 against 180, and the stop step has no horizontal forces at all, so both have zero net force.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the test car only cares about the difference between the forward push and the backward drag, and the torque sensors let the design prove it instead of assuming it. While the push is bigger the car speeds up, while they are equal the car holds its speed, and while the backward force is bigger the car slows down. The demo proves the arithmetic by adding the horizontal forces at each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Start': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 2520 N and Fy = 2.20436e-14 N, against a required net force of Fx = 2520 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 9.09762e-13 N against a tolerance of 4.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.08124e-12 N and Fy = 2.20436e-14 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.08147e-12 N against a tolerance of 4.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Brake': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4680 N and Fy = 5.73135e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -4680 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.07502e-12 N against a tolerance of 4.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.08124e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.08124e-12 N against a tolerance of 4.5 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

F_net = drive - resistance; start: 2700 - 180 = 2520 N; cruise: 180 - 180 = 0 N; brake: -(4500 + 180) = -4680 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

In plain words, the test car only cares about the difference between the forward push and the backward drag, and the torque sensors let the design prove it instead of assuming it. While the push is bigger the car speeds up, while they are equal the car holds its speed, and while the backward force is bigger the car slows down. The demo proves the arithmetic by adding the horizontal forces at each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Proof sketch

Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.08124e-12 N and Fy = 2.20436e-14 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.08147e-12 N against a tolerance of 4.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Brake': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4680 N and Fy = 5.73135e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -4680 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.07502e-12 N against a tolerance of 4.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.08124e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.08124e-12 N against a tolerance of 4.5 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Car Start, Cruise, and Braking Stop'
steps = [('Start', [('Drive force', 2700.0, 0), ('Rolling resistance', 180.0, 180), ('Normal force', 8829.0, 90), ('Gravity', 8829.0, 270)], [2520.0, 0.0], 4.5), ('Cruise', [('Drive force', 180.0, 0), ('Rolling resistance', 180.0, 180), ('Normal force', 8829.0, 90), ('Gravity', 8829.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 4.5), ('Brake', [('Braking force', 4500.0, 180), ('Rolling resistance', 180.0, 180), ('Normal force', 8829.0, 90), ('Gravity', 8829.0, 270)], [-4680.0, 0.0], 4.5), ('Stop', [('Normal force', 8829.0, 90), ('Gravity', 8829.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 4.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Ball Thrown Upward: Rise, Apex, Fall, Bounce Rank 3 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature calibrated ball-throw rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature ball-throw rig: a 0.75 kilogram test ball launched by a calibrated arm, scaled from the public 0.5 kilogram case by a factor of 1.5.

This Signature design study proposes a ball-throw rig for teaching projectile sequences: a 0.75 kilogram ball launched by a calibrated arm, scaled from the public 0.5 kilogram case by a factor of 1.5. The ball weighs 7.35 newtons. The arm pushes up with 30 newtons, so the net force is 22.65 newtons up and the ball leaves fast. In the rise, gravity pulls down with 7.35 newtons and air drag adds 0.75 newtons down, so the net force is 8.1 newtons down. At the apex gravity alone acts, 7.35 newtons down. On the floor the surface pushes up with 75 newtons against the 7.35 newton weight, so the net force is 67.65 newtons up and the ball rebounds.

History

The Signature rig design starts from the same history as the public record, Galileo's falling bodies and Newton's force account, and turns it into hardware: a calibrated throwing arm, a ball, and a marked wall for measuring the apex. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a repeatable thrown-ball sequence, with the launch force set by the arm rather than by hand.

Method

Set the throwing arm to a 30 newton launch push.; Weigh the ball and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Add gravity and drag during the rise and at the apex.; Take the floor's push at the bounce and subtract the weight.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Launch push — forces:
    • Hand push: 30 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 7.35 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 22.65 N; actual net force Fx = 4.86797e-16 N, Fy = 22.65 N; difference 4.86797e-16 N (tolerance 0.75 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Rise — forces:
    • Gravity: 7.35 N at 270 degrees
    • Air drag: 0.75 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -8.1 N; actual net force Fx = -1.48795e-15 N, Fy = -8.1 N; difference 1.48795e-15 N (tolerance 0.75 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Apex — forces:
    • Gravity: 7.35 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -7.35 N; actual net force Fx = -1.35017e-15 N, Fy = -7.35 N; difference 1.35017e-15 N (tolerance 0.75 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Bounce — forces:
    • Ground normal force: 75 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 7.35 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 67.65 N; actual net force Fx = 3.24225e-15 N, Fy = 67.65 N; difference 3.24225e-15 N (tolerance 0.75 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: launch push, rise, apex, bounce. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: arm push 30 newtons in the launch step. A weaker push gives a lower throw; the sequence needs the stated push for the stated flight.

Physics basis

Step 1, launch push: arm push 30 newtons up against gravity 7.35 newtons down gives a net force of 22.65 newtons up. Step 2, rise: gravity 7.35 newtons plus air drag 0.75 newtons downward gives 8.1 newtons down, which slows the ball. Step 3, apex: gravity 7.35 newtons down alone gives 7.35 newtons down at the instant of zero speed. Step 4, bounce: floor push 75 newtons up against gravity 7.35 newtons down gives 67.65 newtons up, which rebounds the ball.

Worked example

A student loads the Signature 0.75 kilogram ball into the throwing arm and fires it straight up. It leaves the arm accelerating upward, slows all the way to the top of its arc, hangs for an instant at the apex against the marked wall, and falls back, speeding up all the way down. It hits the floor, the surface pushes back with 75 newtons, and the ball springs back up for a smaller second throw.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature calibrated ball-throw rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 0.75 times 9.81 is 7.35, 30 minus 7.35 is 22.65 for the launch step, and 7.35 plus 0.75 is 8.1 for the rise step. The bounce step is 75 minus 7.35, which is 67.65, matching the stated net force.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the arm only matters for a moment; after the ball leaves the arm, gravity takes over and never lets go. It slows the ball on the way up, stops it for an instant at the top, and speeds it up on the way down. The bounce is the floor pushing back harder than gravity pulls, which is what sends the ball up again. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Launch push': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 4.86797e-16 N and Fy = 22.65 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 22.65 N; the difference is 4.86797e-16 N against a tolerance of 0.75 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Rise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.48795e-15 N and Fy = -8.1 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -8.1 N; the difference is 1.48795e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.75 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Apex': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.35017e-15 N and Fy = -7.35 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -7.35 N; the difference is 1.35017e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.75 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Bounce': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 3.24225e-15 N and Fy = 67.65 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 67.65 N; the difference is 3.24225e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.75 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Launch: 30 - 7.35 = 22.65 N up; rise: -(7.35 + 0.75) = -8.1 N; apex: -7.35 N; bounce: 75 - 7.35 = 67.65 N up (stated, not machine-checked)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Ball Thrown Upward: Rise, Apex, Fall, Bounce'
steps = [('Launch push', [('Hand push', 30.0, 90), ('Gravity', 7.35, 270)], [0.0, 22.65], 0.75), ('Rise', [('Gravity', 7.35, 270), ('Air drag', 0.75, 270)], [0.0, -8.1], 0.75), ('Apex', [('Gravity', 7.35, 270)], [0.0, -7.35], 0.75), ('Bounce', [('Ground normal force', 75.0, 90), ('Gravity', 7.35, 270)], [0.0, 67.65], 0.75)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Spring Compression, Release, Oscillation, Settle Rank 4 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature spring test bench design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature spring test bench: a 4 kilogram mass on a spring of stiffness 100 newtons per metre, scaled from the public 5 kilogram case by a factor of 0.8.

This Signature design study proposes a spring test bench for teaching oscillation sequences: a 4 kilogram mass on a spring of stiffness 100 newtons per metre, scaled from the public 5 kilogram case by a factor of 0.8. The mass weighs 39.24 newtons. Held compressed, the hand pushes down with 24 newtons, the spring pushes up with 63.24 newtons, and gravity pulls down with 39.24 newtons, so the net force is zero. On release the spring's 63.24 newtons up beats gravity's 39.24 newtons down, and the net force is 24 newtons up. Passing equilibrium the spring force is 39.24 newtons, exactly matching gravity, so the net force is zero. At rest the same balance holds, and the mass settles.

History

The Signature bench design starts from the same history as the public record, Hooke's 1678 spring law and the mass-spring oscillator, and turns it into a bench with a scale beside the spring so the stretch can be read at every step. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a measured compression cycle, with the spring force read off the scale rather than assumed.

Method

Mount the spring on the bench and hang the 4 kilogram test mass.; Read the stretch on the bench scale and compute the spring force.; Add hand, spring, and gravity at the held step and check the sum is zero.; Remove the hand force for the release step and recompute the net force.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Compress — forces:
    • Hand push: 24 N at 270 degrees
    • Spring force: 63.24 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 39.24 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -7.74467e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 7.74467e-15 N (tolerance 0.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Release — forces:
    • Spring force: 63.24 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 39.24 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 24 N; actual net force Fx = -3.33594e-15 N, Fy = 24 N; difference 3.33594e-15 N (tolerance 0.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Oscillation — forces:
    • Spring force: 39.24 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 39.24 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -4.80551e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 4.80551e-15 N (tolerance 0.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Settle — forces:
    • Spring force: 39.24 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 39.24 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -4.80551e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 4.80551e-15 N (tolerance 0.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: compress, release, oscillation, settle. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: spring force 63.24 newtons at the compressed stretch. The spring must stay inside its elastic range or the law stops applying.

Physics basis

Step 1, compress: spring force 63.24 newtons up against hand push 24 newtons down and gravity 39.24 newtons down gives zero net force. Step 2, release: spring force 63.24 newtons up against gravity 39.24 newtons down gives 24 newtons up, which launches the mass. Step 3, oscillation: spring force 39.24 newtons up against gravity 39.24 newtons down gives zero net force at the equilibrium point. Step 4, settle: the same balance holds at rest, so the mass stays put.

Worked example

A student presses the Signature 4 kilogram mass down on the bench spring until the spring force reads 63.24 newtons, holding it with a 24 newton push. Let go, and the mass leaps upward, overshoots the equilibrium point, swings back down, and oscillates smaller and smaller until it hangs still at the stretch where the spring force of 39.24 newtons exactly matches the weight.

Evidence

Verification path

Check by hand: 4 times 9.81 is 39.24, and 63.24 minus 39.24 minus 24 is zero for the compress step, while 63.24 minus 39.24 is 24 for the release step. At equilibrium the spring force is 39.24 newtons, matching the weight exactly.

Science proof

In plain words, the bench spring is a force that grows as it is stretched, so it automatically finds the stretch where it matches the weight, and any push away from that stretch is met by a force pushing back. Release the mass and the extra spring force throws it upward; pass the balance point and gravity takes over. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Step 'Release': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3.33594e-15 N and Fy = 24 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 24 N; the difference is 3.33594e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Oscillation': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4.80551e-15 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 4.80551e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Settle': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4.80551e-15 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 4.80551e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.4 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Spring Compression, Release, Oscillation, Settle'
steps = [('Compress', [('Hand push', 24.0, 270), ('Spring force', 63.24, 90), ('Gravity', 39.24, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.4), ('Release', [('Spring force', 63.24, 90), ('Gravity', 39.24, 270)], [0.0, 24.0], 0.4), ('Oscillation', [('Spring force', 39.24, 90), ('Gravity', 39.24, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.4), ('Settle', [('Spring force', 39.24, 90), ('Gravity', 39.24, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.4)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Crate Push: Break Static Friction, Slide, Stop Rank 5 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature measured crate-push rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature crate-push rig: an 11 kilogram test crate on a measured floor, scaled from the public 10 kilogram case by a factor of 1.1.

This Signature design study proposes a crate-push rig for measuring friction sequences: an 11 kilogram crate on a floor with known friction coefficients, scaled from the public 10 kilogram case by a factor of 1.1. The crate weighs 107.91 newtons. At first static friction grips with up to 53.95 newtons, from a coefficient of 0.5 times the normal force, and a 55 newton push beats it by 1.04 newtons, so the crate breaks free. Once sliding, kinetic friction is only 32.37 newtons, from a coefficient of 0.3, so the same 55 newton push leaves 22.63 newtons forward and the crate accelerates. Stop pushing and kinetic friction alone, 32.37 newtons backward, slows the crate to a stop. At rest the push is gone, friction is zero, and the crate stays put.

History

The Signature rig design starts from the same history as the public record, Amontons and Coulomb and the two friction coefficients, and turns it into a measured floor: a push handle with a force gauge and a marked track for timing the slide. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a measured break-free sequence, with the push read off the gauge rather than guessed.

Method

Place the 11 kilogram test crate on the measured floor.; Read the push from the force gauge at the break-free step.; Compute the maximum static friction from the static coefficient.; Compute kinetic friction from the kinetic coefficient for the sliding steps.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Break free — forces:
    • Applied push: 55 N at 0 degrees
    • Static friction: 53.95 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 107.91 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 107.91 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 1.04 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 1.05 N, Fy = 6.60697e-15 N; difference 0.01 N (tolerance 0.55 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Slide — forces:
    • Applied push: 55 N at 0 degrees
    • Kinetic friction: 32.37 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 107.91 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 107.91 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 22.63 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 22.63 N, Fy = 3.96418e-15 N; difference 1.13715e-14 N (tolerance 0.55 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Stop pushing — forces:
    • Kinetic friction: 32.37 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 107.91 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 107.91 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -32.37 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -32.37 N, Fy = 3.96418e-15 N; difference 1.47534e-14 N (tolerance 0.55 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: At rest — forces:
    • Normal force: 107.91 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 107.91 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.32152e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.32152e-14 N (tolerance 0.55 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: break free, slide, stop pushing, at rest. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: static friction limit 53.95 newtons. A push below it never starts the crate, and the sequence never begins.

Physics basis

Step 1, break free: push 55 newtons forward against static friction 53.95 newtons backward gives a net force of 1.04 newtons forward, which starts the crate moving. Step 2, slide: push 55 newtons against kinetic friction 32.37 newtons gives 22.63 newtons forward, which accelerates the crate. Step 3, stop pushing: kinetic friction 32.37 newtons backward alone gives 32.37 newtons backward, which slows the crate. Step 4, at rest: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the crate stays put.

Worked example

A worker pushes the Signature 11 kilogram crate with 55 newtons on the gauge. At first nothing happens, then the crate jerks free as the push passes the 53.95 newton static limit. It slides more easily than expected because kinetic friction is only 32.37 newtons, so the crate accelerates. The worker stops pushing, friction alone slows the crate, and it comes to rest a few metres on.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature measured crate-push rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 0.5 times 107.91 is 53.95, so 55 minus 53.95 is 1.04 for the break-free step, and 0.3 times 107.91 is 32.37, so 55 minus 32.37 is 22.63 for the slide step. The stopping step is friction alone, 32.37 newtons backward.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, friction is stronger before things move than while they move, so the hardest part of pushing a crate is the first centimetre, and the gauge on the rig proves it by reading the push at the exact moment the crate breaks free. Once it slides, the same push has more force left over, which is why the crate suddenly speeds up. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Break free': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 1.05 N and Fy = 6.60697e-15 N, against a required net force of Fx = 1.04 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 0.01 N against a tolerance of 0.55 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Slide': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 22.63 N and Fy = 3.96418e-15 N, against a required net force of Fx = 22.63 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.13715e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.55 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop pushing': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -32.37 N and Fy = 3.96418e-15 N, against a required net force of Fx = -32.37 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.47534e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.55 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'At rest': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.32152e-14 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.32152e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.55 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Static limit = mu_s * N = 0.5 * 107.91 = 53.95 N; kinetic friction = mu_k * N = 0.3 * 107.91 = 32.37 N; slide net = 55 - 32.37 = 22.63 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Pendulum Release, Swing, Damp, Rest Rank 6 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature instrumented pendulum rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature pendulum rig: a 13 kilogram bob on a 1 metre string, scaled from the public 10 kilogram case by a factor of 1.3.

This Signature design study proposes a pendulum rig for teaching swing sequences: a 13 kilogram bob on a 1 metre string, scaled from the public 10 kilogram case by a factor of 1.3. The bob weighs 127.53 newtons. Released at 30 degrees from vertical, the string tension is 110.45 newtons along the string, and with gravity the net force is 63.77 newtons along the swing, which is mass times gravity times the sine of 30 degrees. At the bottom the tension is 153.04 newtons up against gravity 127.53 newtons down, so the net force is 25.51 newtons up, exactly the centripetal force the curved path needs. On the other side the same net force acts the other way, and at rest the tension is 127.53 newtons, matching the weight.

History

The Signature rig design starts from the same history as the public record, Galileo's timed swings and Huygens's pendulum clock of 1656, and turns it into a rig with a protractor arc and a tension gauge on the string. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a measured pendulum swing, with the tension read off the gauge at the bottom of every swing.

Method

Hang the 13 kilogram bob on the 1 metre string and fit the tension gauge.; Pull the bob aside to 30 degrees on the protractor arc and release it.; Resolve the tension along the string at the release angle.; Read the tension gauge at the bottom of the swing and compare with the centripetal requirement.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Release — forces:
    • Tension: 110.45 N at 60 degrees
    • Gravity: 127.53 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 55.22 N, Fy = -31.88 N; actual net force Fx = 55.225 N, Fy = -31.8775 N; difference 0.00559279 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Bottom of swing — forces:
    • Tension: 153.04 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 127.53 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 25.51 N; actual net force Fx = -1.40559e-14 N, Fy = 25.51 N; difference 1.76398e-14 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Rise — forces:
    • Tension: 110.45 N at 120 degrees
    • Gravity: 127.53 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -55.22 N, Fy = -31.88 N; actual net force Fx = -55.225 N, Fy = -31.8775 N; difference 0.00559279 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Rest — forces:
    • Tension: 127.53 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 127.53 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.56179e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.56179e-14 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: release, bottom of swing, rise, rest. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: string tension 153.04 newtons at the bottom of the swing. A string rated below the needed tension snaps at the fastest point.

Physics basis

Step 1, release: tension 110.45 newtons along the string at 60 degrees against gravity 127.53 newtons down gives a net force of 63.77 newtons along the swing. Step 2, bottom of swing: tension 153.04 newtons up against gravity 127.53 newtons down gives 25.51 newtons up, the centripetal force the arc requires. Step 3, rise: tension 110.45 newtons at 120 degrees against gravity gives 63.77 newtons along the swing the other way. Step 4, rest: tension 127.53 newtons up against gravity 127.53 newtons down gives zero net force.

Worked example

A student pulls the Signature 13 kilogram bob aside to 30 degrees and lets go. It sweeps down, moving fastest at the bottom where the tension gauge reads 153.04 newtons, climbs the other side to nearly the same height, and swings back. Each swing is a little smaller than the last as air drag and the pivot eat the energy, until the bob hangs still at the bottom with the tension equal to its weight.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature instrumented pendulum rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 13 times 9.81 times sin 30 degrees is 63.77, matching the release step, and the bottom step tension of 153.04 minus the weight of 127.53 is 25.51, matching the stated net force.

Science proof

In plain words, the rig shows the same lesson as the public record with numbers the student can read: at the sides of the swing gravity has a component along the arc that speeds the bob up or slows it down, and at the bottom the string must pull harder than gravity by exactly the amount the curve demands. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Release': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 55.225 N and Fy = -31.8775 N, against a required net force of Fx = 55.22 N and Fy = -31.88 N; the difference is 0.00559279 N against a tolerance of 0.65 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Bottom of swing': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.40559e-14 N and Fy = 25.51 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 25.51 N; the difference is 1.76398e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.65 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Rise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -55.225 N and Fy = -31.8775 N, against a required net force of Fx = -55.22 N and Fy = -31.88 N; the difference is 0.00559279 N against a tolerance of 0.65 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Rest': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.56179e-14 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.56179e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.65 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Release net = m * g * sin(30) = 63.77 N; bottom tension = m * g + m * v^2 / L = 127.53 + 25.51 = 153.04 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

In plain words, the rig shows the same lesson as the public record with numbers the student can read: at the sides of the swing gravity has a component along the arc that speeds the bob up or slows it down, and at the bottom the string must pull harder than gravity by exactly the amount the curve demands. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Pendulum Release, Swing, Damp, Rest'
steps = [('Release', [('Tension', 110.45, 60), ('Gravity', 127.53, 270)], [55.22, -31.88], 0.65), ('Bottom of swing', [('Tension', 153.04, 90), ('Gravity', 127.53, 270)], [0.0, 25.51], 0.65), ('Rise', [('Tension', 110.45, 120), ('Gravity', 127.53, 270)], [-55.22, -31.88], 0.65), ('Rest', [('Tension', 127.53, 90), ('Gravity', 127.53, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.65)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Rowing Stroke: Catch, Drive, Release, Recovery Rank 7 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature instrumented rowing shell design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature rowing shell: a 90 kilogram boat with oar force gauges, scaled from the public 100 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9.

This Signature design study proposes an instrumented rowing shell for measuring stroke sequences: a 90 kilogram boat with force gauges on the oars, scaled from the public 100 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9. The boat weighs 882.9 newtons, held up by the same buoyancy. At the catch the oars pull with 360 newtons against 90 newtons of water drag, so the net force is 270 newtons forward. In the drive the oars pull with 450 newtons against 135 newtons of drag, so the net force is 315 newtons forward. At the release the oars leave the water and drag alone, 135 newtons backward, slows the boat. In the recovery the rower slides forward while the boat glides against 45 newtons of drag.

History

The Signature shell design starts from the same history as the public record, rowing from ancient galleys to the Boat Race of 1829, and turns it into a measuring boat: oarlocks with force gauges and a speed log. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a measured stroke cycle, with the oar force recorded at every phase instead of estimated.

Method

Build the 90 kilogram shell and fit force gauges to the oarlocks.; Measure the oar force and the water drag at each step.; Add the horizontal forces at each step to get the net force.; Compare each step's net force with the net force that step's motion requires.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Catch — forces:
    • Oar force: 360 N at 0 degrees
    • Water drag: 90 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 882.9 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 882.9 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 270 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 270 N, Fy = 1.10218e-14 N; difference 1.1422e-13 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Drive — forces:
    • Oar force: 450 N at 0 degrees
    • Water drag: 135 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 882.9 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 882.9 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 315 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 315 N, Fy = 1.65327e-14 N; difference 1.14883e-13 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Release — forces:
    • Water drag: 135 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 882.9 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 882.9 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -135 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -135 N, Fy = 1.65327e-14 N; difference 1.14883e-13 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Recovery — forces:
    • Water drag: 45 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 882.9 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 882.9 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -45 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -45 N, Fy = 5.51091e-15 N; difference 1.06724e-13 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: catch, drive, release, recovery. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: oar force 450 newtons in the drive step. A weaker drive gains less speed than the glide loses, and the boat never gets up to speed.

Physics basis

Step 1, catch: oar force 360 newtons forward against water drag 90 newtons backward gives a net force of 270 newtons forward. Step 2, drive: oar force 450 newtons against drag 135 newtons gives 315 newtons forward. Step 3, release: drag 135 newtons backward alone gives 135 newtons backward, which slows the boat. Step 4, recovery: drag 45 newtons backward alone gives 45 newtons backward while the boat glides.

Worked example

A rower in the Signature shell takes a stroke on a calm river. The gauges read 360 newtons at the catch and 450 newtons in the drive, and the speed log shows the boat surging forward. The blades lift out, the boat glides and slows gently, and the rower slides forward on the seat for the next catch, the gauges reading zero until the blades bite again.

Verification path

Check by hand: 360 minus 90 is 270 for the catch step, and 450 minus 135 is 315 for the drive step. The release and recovery steps are drag alone, 135 and 45 newtons backward, and the vertical forces cancel at every step, 882.9 against 882.9.

Science proof

In plain words, the shell only moves when the oars pull harder than the water drags, and the gauges let the design prove it at every phase of the stroke. The moment the oars leave the water the drag starts winning and the boat slows, and the stroke works because the drive is strong and the recovery is gentle. unit = 'N'

    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

Seesaw Sequence: Load, Balance, Shift, Rebalance Rank 8 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature calibrated seesaw rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature seesaw rig: a calibrated lever with test weights, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.4.

This Signature design study proposes a calibrated seesaw rig for teaching balance sequences: a lever with test weights and a pivot load cell, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.4. At loading, a 420 newton weight on the left and a 280 newton weight on the right weigh 700 newtons together, so the pivot pushes up with 700 newtons and the net force is zero. When the right side is matched at 420 newtons, the total is 840 newtons and the pivot pushes with 840 newtons. When the right side grows to 560 newtons, the total is 980 newtons and the pivot pushes with 980 newtons. Rebalanced at 490 newtons a side, the total is still 980 newtons and the pivot still pushes with 980 newtons, so the lever sits level.

History

The Signature rig design starts from the same history as the public record, Archimedes and the lever principle of the third century BCE, and turns it into a calibrated instrument: a lever, a set of test weights, and a load cell under the pivot. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a measured balance sequence, with the pivot force read off the cell at every step.

Method

Set the test weights on the lever and read the pivot load cell.; Add the weights to get the total load at each step.; Set the pivot force equal to the total load at each step.; Check that the sum is zero, which is the balance condition.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Load — forces:
    • Left child: 420 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 280 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 700 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -8.57253e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 8.57253e-14 N (tolerance 0.7 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Balance — forces:
    • Left child: 420 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 420 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 840 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.0287e-13 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.0287e-13 N (tolerance 0.7 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Shift — forces:
    • Left child: 420 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 560 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 980 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20015e-13 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20015e-13 N (tolerance 0.7 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Rebalance — forces:
    • Left child: 490 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 490 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 980 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20015e-13 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20015e-13 N (tolerance 0.7 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: load, balance, shift, rebalance. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: pivot force 980 newtons in the shift and rebalance steps. A pivot rated below the total load cannot hold the lever up.

Physics basis

Step 1, load: left weight 420 newtons down and right weight 280 newtons down against pivot force 700 newtons up gives zero net force. Step 2, balance: 420 and 420 newtons down against pivot force 840 newtons up gives zero net force. Step 3, shift: 420 and 560 newtons down against pivot force 980 newtons up gives zero net force. Step 4, rebalance: 490 and 490 newtons down against pivot force 980 newtons up gives zero net force.

Worked example

A student loads the Signature seesaw rig with a 420 newton weight on the left and a 280 newton weight on the right, and the load cell reads 700 newtons while the lever tips gently to the heavy side. The student adds weight to the right until both sides carry 420 newtons, the cell reads 840 newtons, and the lever levels. A further weight takes the right side to 560 newtons, the cell reads 980 newtons, and the student rebalances at 490 newtons a side.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature calibrated seesaw rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 420 plus 280 is 700, 420 plus 420 is 840, 420 plus 560 is 980, and 490 plus 490 is 980, and in each step the pivot force equals the total, so every net force is zero.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the pivot does not decide the balance, the weights do; the pivot only has to push up exactly as hard as all the weights together weigh down, or the whole lever would fall, and the load cell shows that number changing at every step. When a weight moves, the total changes, and the pivot force has to change with it. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Load': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -8.57253e-14 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 8.57253e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.7 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Balance': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.0287e-13 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.0287e-13 N against a tolerance of 0.7 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Shift': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20015e-13 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20015e-13 N against a tolerance of 0.7 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Rebalance': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20015e-13 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20015e-13 N against a tolerance of 0.7 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Pivot force = sum of weights; load: 420 + 280 = 700 N; balance: 420 + 420 = 840 N; shift: 420 + 560 = 980 N; rebalance: 490 + 490 = 980 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Seesaw Sequence: Load, Balance, Shift, Rebalance'
steps = [('Load', [('Left child', 420.0, 270), ('Right child', 280.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 700.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.7), ('Balance', [('Left child', 420.0, 270), ('Right child', 420.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 840.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.7), ('Shift', [('Left child', 420.0, 270), ('Right child', 560.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 980.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.7), ('Rebalance', [('Left child', 490.0, 270), ('Right child', 490.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 980.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.7)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Model Rocket: Thrust, Coast, Apogee, Parachute Descent, Touchdown Rank 9 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature sized model rocket design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature model rocket: a 1.2 kilogram rocket with a larger motor and parachute, scaled from the public 1 kilogram case by a factor of 1.2.

This Signature design study proposes a model rocket with a larger motor and a sized parachute: a 1.2 kilogram rocket, scaled from the public 1 kilogram case by a factor of 1.2. The rocket weighs 11.77 newtons. Under thrust the motor pushes up with 36 newtons against gravity and 0.23 newtons of drag, so the net force is 24 newtons up. Coasting, gravity and 1.2 newtons of drag pull down with 12.97 newtons, so the rocket slows. At apogee gravity alone pulls down with 11.77 newtons at the instant of zero speed. Under the parachute, drag pushes up with 11.77 newtons against the weight, so the net force is zero and the rocket falls at a steady, survivable speed. At touchdown the ground pushes up with 11.77 newtons and the rocket stands still.

History

The Signature rocket design starts from the same history as the public record, Carlisle and Stine's hobby motors of the late 1950s, and turns it into a sized design: a 1.2 kilogram airframe, a motor chosen to give a 24 newton net thrust, and a parachute sized so its drag matches the weight. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a rocket whose every step is calculated before it flies.

Method

Weigh the rocket and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Size the motor so thrust minus gravity and drag gives the planned net force.; Add gravity and drag for the coast and apogee steps.; Size the parachute so its drag equals the weight for the descent step.; Run the demo, which checks all five steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Thrust — forces:
    • Thrust: 36 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 11.77 N at 270 degrees
    • Air drag: 0.23 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 24 N; actual net force Fx = 4.62223e-31 N, Fy = 24 N; difference 4.62223e-31 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Coast — forces:
    • Gravity: 11.77 N at 270 degrees
    • Air drag: 1.2 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -12.97 N; actual net force Fx = -2.38255e-15 N, Fy = -12.97 N; difference 2.97187e-15 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Apogee — forces:
    • Gravity: 11.77 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -11.77 N; actual net force Fx = -2.16211e-15 N, Fy = -11.77 N; difference 2.16211e-15 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Parachute descent — forces:
    • Parachute drag: 11.77 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 11.77 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.44141e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.44141e-15 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  5. Step 5: Touchdown — forces:
    • Ground normal force: 11.77 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 11.77 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.44141e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.44141e-15 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: thrust, coast, apogee, parachute descent, touchdown. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the parachute must open at apogee. If it fails, the descent step has drag far below the weight and the rocket falls at speed.

Physics basis

Step 1, thrust: motor thrust 36 newtons up against gravity 11.77 newtons and drag 0.23 newtons down gives a net force of 24 newtons up. Step 2, coast: gravity 11.77 newtons plus drag 1.2 newtons down gives 12.97 newtons down, which slows the rocket. Step 3, apogee: gravity 11.77 newtons down alone gives 11.77 newtons down at zero speed. Step 4, parachute descent: drag 11.77 newtons up against gravity 11.77 newtons down gives zero net force. Step 5, touchdown: ground push 11.77 newtons up against gravity 11.77 newtons down gives zero net force.

Worked example

A student launches the Signature 1.2 kilogram rocket from a school field. The motor burns, the rocket streaks upward, and then it coasts silently to its highest point, where a small charge pops the sized parachute out. The rocket drifts down under the canopy at a few metres per second and touches down softly in the grass, ready to fly again.

Evidence
