Verification path

Check by hand: 36 minus 11.77 minus 0.23 is 24 for the thrust step, and 11.77 plus 1.2 is 12.97 for the coast step. The parachute step is an exact balance, 11.77 against 11.77, so its net force is zero.

Science proof

In plain words, the motor only fires for a moment, and the rocket spends most of its flight coasting on the speed the motor gave it, slowing all the way up. The parachute works by making drag as big as the weight, so the rocket stops speeding up and falls gently instead of crashing, and the design sizes the canopy to that exact number. import json
import math

title = 'Model Rocket: Thrust, Coast, Apogee, Parachute Descent, Touchdown'
steps = [('Thrust', [('Thrust', 36.0, 90), ('Gravity', 11.77, 270), ('Air drag', 0.23, 270)], [0.0, 24.0], 0.6), ('Coast', [('Gravity', 11.77, 270), ('Air drag', 1.2, 270)], [0.0, -12.97], 0.6), ('Apogee', [('Gravity', 11.77, 270)], [0.0, -11.77], 0.6), ('Parachute descent', [('Parachute drag', 11.77, 90), ('Gravity', 11.77, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.6), ('Touchdown', [('Ground normal force', 11.77, 90), ('Gravity', 11.77, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.6)]
unit = 'N'

    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")

Aircraft Takeoff, Climb, Cruise, Descent, Landing Rank 10 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature light aircraft design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature light aircraft: an 80,000 newton aircraft design, scaled from the public 100,000 newton case by a factor of 0.8.

This Signature design study proposes a light aircraft with a full flight profile: an 80,000 newton aircraft, scaled from the public 100,000 newton case by a factor of 0.8. On the takeoff roll, thrust of 24,000 newtons beats drag of 4000 newtons while lift of 48,000 newtons is still short of the weight, so the net force is 20,000 newtons forward and 32,000 newtons down and the aircraft stays on the runway, accelerating. In the climb, lift of 88,000 newtons beats the weight and the net force is 12,000 newtons forward and 8000 newtons up. In cruise, thrust matches drag at 12,000 newtons and lift matches weight at 80,000 newtons, so the net force is zero. In descent the net force is 3200 newtons backward and 4000 newtons down, and in the landing flare it is 2400 newtons backward with lift matching weight.

History

The Signature aircraft design starts from the same history as the public record, the Wright brothers in 1903 and the four forces of flight, and turns it into a sized design: a wing sized to lift 80,000 newtons and engines sized to the thrust the profile needs. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a light aircraft whose whole flight is calculated step by step before it flies.

Method

Take the aircraft weight and the four forces at each step.; Add thrust and drag horizontally and lift and weight vertically at each step.; Compare each step's net force with the net force that step's motion requires.; Confirm the cruise step has zero net force in both directions.; Run the demo, which checks all five steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Takeoff roll — forces:
    • Thrust: 24000 N at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 4000 N at 180 degrees
    • Lift: 48000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 80000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 20000 N, Fy = -32000 N; actual net force Fx = 20000 N, Fy = -32000 N; difference 1.09139e-11 N (tolerance 4 N); step verdict HOLDS.

    • Lift: 88000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 80000 N at 270 degrees

    • Thrust: 12000 N at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 12000 N at 180 degrees

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: takeoff roll, climb, cruise, descent, landing flare. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: lift must reach the weight before rotation, and thrust must match drag in cruise. Either shortfall breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, takeoff roll: thrust 24,000 newtons against drag 4000 newtons gives 20,000 newtons forward, and lift 48,000 newtons against weight 80,000 newtons gives 32,000 newtons down, so the aircraft stays on the runway while accelerating. Step 2, climb: thrust 20,000 against drag 8000 gives 12,000 newtons forward, and lift 88,000 against weight 80,000 gives 8000 newtons up. Step 3, cruise: thrust 12,000 against drag 12,000 and lift 80,000 against weight 80,000 give zero net force. Step 4, descent: thrust 6400 against drag 9600 gives 3200 newtons backward, and lift 76,000 against weight 80,000 gives 4000 newtons down. Step 5, landing flare: thrust 4000 against drag 6400 gives 2400 newtons backward, and lift 80,000 against weight 80,000 gives zero vertical net force.

Worked example

The Signature light aircraft accelerates down the runway, its nose rising as lift grows past the weight, and it climbs away from the ground. At cruising altitude the engines settle to the thrust that exactly matches drag, and the wing lifts exactly the weight, so the aircraft flies level for hours. On approach the thrust drops, the aircraft descends, and in the flare the lift matches the weight again just as the wheels touch the runway.

Verification path

Check by hand: 24,000 minus 4000 is 20,000 and 48,000 minus 80,000 is minus 32,000 for the takeoff step, while 20,000 minus 8000 is 12,000 and 88,000 minus 80,000 is 8000 for the climb step. The cruise step is an exact balance in both directions.

Science proof

In plain words, the design only flies when its wing lifts as hard as gravity pulls, and it only moves forward when its engines push as hard as the air drags back. On the runway the wing is not lifting enough yet, so the aircraft stays down while it speeds up; in the air the balance is struck, and the aircraft cruises. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 5 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Takeoff roll': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 20000 N and Fy = -32000 N, against a required net force of Fx = 20000 N and Fy = -32000 N; the difference is 1.09139e-11 N against a tolerance of 4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Climb': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 12000 N and Fy = 8000 N, against a required net force of Fx = 12000 N and Fy = 8000 N; the difference is 9.14031e-12 N against a tolerance of 4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -9.79717e-12 N and Fy = 1.46958e-12 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 9.90678e-12 N against a tolerance of 4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Descent': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3200 N and Fy = -4000 N, against a required net force of Fx = -3200 N and Fy = -4000 N; the difference is 1.0097e-11 N against a tolerance of 4 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Aircraft Takeoff, Climb, Cruise, Descent, Landing'
steps = [('Takeoff roll', [('Thrust', 24000.0, 0), ('Drag', 4000.0, 180), ('Lift', 48000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 80000.0, 270)], [20000.0, -32000.0], 4.0), ('Climb', [('Thrust', 20000.0, 0), ('Drag', 8000.0, 180), ('Lift', 88000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 80000.0, 270)], [12000.0, 8000.0], 4.0), ('Cruise', [('Thrust', 12000.0, 0), ('Drag', 12000.0, 180), ('Lift', 80000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 80000.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 4.0), ('Descent', [('Thrust', 6400.0, 0), ('Drag', 9600.0, 180), ('Lift', 76000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 80000.0, 270)], [-3200.0, -4000.0], 4.0), ('Landing flare', [('Thrust', 4000.0, 0), ('Drag', 6400.0, 180), ('Lift', 80000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 80000.0, 270)], [-2400.0, 0.0], 4.0)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Satellite Launch: Thrust, Gravity Turn, Circularization, Orbit Rank 11 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature satellite launcher design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature satellite launcher: an 1100 kilogram satellite design, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.1.

This Signature design study proposes a satellite launcher for an 1100 kilogram satellite, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.1. The satellite weighs 10,791 newtons on the ground. In the vertical thrust step the engine pushes up with 16,500 newtons, so the net force is 5709 newtons up. In the gravity turn the engine pushes 13,200 newtons at 45 degrees while gravity pulls down with 9900 newtons, so the net force is 9333.81 newtons forward and 566.19 newtons down, bending the path sideways. The circularization burn adds 110 newtons forward while gravity pulls down with 10,153 newtons at orbital height. In orbit, gravity of 10,153 newtons toward the Earth plus a whisper of drag, 0.01 newtons, is the centripetal force the circular path requires.

History

The Signature launcher design starts from the same history as the public record, Newton's Principia and Sputnik 1 in 1957, and turns it into a sized design: a rocket sized to lift 1100 kilograms, a gravity-turn programme, and a circularization burn sized to the orbital balance. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a launcher whose whole ascent is calculated step by step before it flies.

Method

Take the satellite mass and the thrust at each step.; Resolve the gravity-turn thrust into forward and vertical parts.; Add thrust and gravity at each step to get the net force.; Compare the orbit step's net force with the centripetal force the orbit requires.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Worked example

The Signature rocket lifts its 1100 kilogram satellite straight up from the pad, then leans over into a gravity turn, its thrust bending the path sideways while gravity keeps pulling down. At orbital height a final burn circularizes the path, the engine cuts out, and the satellite circles the Earth, falling around the planet and missing the ground, with only a whisper of air drag slowly decaying its orbit.

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Satellite Launch: Thrust, Gravity Turn, Circularization, Orbit'
steps = [('Thrust', [('Thrust', 16500.0, 90), ('Gravity', 10791.0, 270)], [0.0, 5709.0], 5.5), ('Gravity turn', [('Thrust', 13200.0, 45), ('Gravity', 9900.0, 270)], [9333.81, -566.19], 1.1), ('Circularization', [('Engine thrust', 110.0, 0), ('Gravity', 10153.0, 270)], [110.0, -10153.0], 5.5), ('Orbit', [('Gravity', 10153.0, 270), ('Atmospheric drag', 0.01, 180)], [0.0, -10153.0], 5.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)

Bungee Jump: Free Fall, Cord Stretch, Rebound, Settle Rank 12 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature sized bungee rig design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature bungee rig: a 104 kilogram test jumper on a sized cord, scaled from the public 80 kilogram case by a factor of 1.3.

This Signature design study proposes a bungee rig with a cord sized for a heavier jumper: a 104 kilogram test mass, scaled from the public 80 kilogram case by a factor of 1.3. The mass weighs 1020.24 newtons. In free fall gravity alone pulls down with 1020.24 newtons. As the cord stretches it pulls up with 1560 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 539.76 newtons up and the fall slows, then reverses. On the rebound the cord pulls with 1170 newtons, so the net force is 149.76 newtons up and the mass rises again. After a few smaller bounces the cord pulls with 1020.24 newtons, exactly matching the weight, so the net force is zero and the mass hangs still.

History

The Signature rig design starts from the same history as the public record, the Oxford club jump of 1979 and the land-diving ritual of Pentecost Island, and turns it into a test rig: a drop tower, a sized cord, and a test mass, so the stretch and rebound can be measured without a person on the line. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a bungee sequence whose cord is sized by calculation, not by guess.

Method

Weigh the test mass and compute the weight from mass times gravity.; Size the cord so its stretch force exceeds the weight with margin.; Take the cord force at each step and subtract the weight.; Confirm the settle step has the cord force exactly matching the weight.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Free fall — forces:
    • Gravity: 1020.24 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -1020.24 N; actual net force Fx = -1.87415e-13 N, Fy = -1020.24 N; difference 1.87415e-13 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: free fall, cord stretch, rebound, settle. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the cord must stretch enough to supply more than the weight before the mass reaches the ground. Too short a cord, or too weak, breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, free fall: gravity 1020.24 newtons down alone gives a net force of 1020.24 newtons down. Step 2, cord stretch: cord force 1560 newtons up against gravity 1020.24 newtons down gives 539.76 newtons up, which slows and reverses the fall. Step 3, rebound: cord force 1170 newtons up against gravity 1020.24 newtons down gives 149.76 newtons up, which lifts the mass again. Step 4, settle: cord force 1020.24 newtons up against gravity 1020.24 newtons down gives zero net force and the mass hangs still.

Worked example

The Signature 104 kilogram test mass drops from the tower and falls freely, speeding up under gravity alone. The cord goes taut, stretches, and pulls up harder than the weight, so the fall slows, stops, and turns into a rebound upward. The mass bounces a few times, each bounce smaller than the last, and finally hangs still beneath the tower with the cord holding exactly its weight.

Science proof

In plain words, the cord is a spring: it does nothing until it is stretched, then it pulls harder the further it stretches, until it is pulling harder than gravity and the fall turns around. The test mass bounces because the cord keeps overshooting the balance point, and settles because each bounce loses a little energy. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Step 'Cord stretch': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -9.18926e-14 N and Fy = 539.76 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 539.76 N; the difference is 9.18926e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.65 N, so the step HOLDS. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Bungee Jump: Free Fall, Cord Stretch, Rebound, Settle'
steps = [('Free fall', [('Gravity', 1020.24, 270)], [0.0, -1020.24], 0.65), ('Cord stretch', [('Cord force', 1560.0, 90), ('Gravity', 1020.24, 270)], [0.0, 539.76], 0.65), ('Rebound', [('Cord force', 1170.0, 90), ('Gravity', 1020.24, 270)], [0.0, 149.76], 0.65), ('Settle', [('Cord force', 1020.24, 90), ('Gravity', 1020.24, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.65)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Roller Coaster: Lift Hill, Drop, Loop, Brake Run Rank 13 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature coaster car design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature coaster car: a 450 kilogram car design, scaled from the public 500 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9.

This Signature design study proposes a coaster car for a compact ride: a 450 kilogram car, scaled from the public 500 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9. The car weighs 4414.5 newtons. On the lift hill the chain pulls up with 5400 newtons against gravity and 180 newtons of friction, so the net force is 805.5 newtons up and the car climbs slowly. On the drop gravity pulls down with 4414.5 newtons plus 85.5 newtons of drag, so the net force is 4500 newtons down and the car plunges. At the top of the loop the track pushes down with 2700 newtons on top of gravity's 4414.5, so the net force is 7114.5 newtons down, the centripetal force the loop requires. On the brake run the brakes push backward with 1800 newtons while the track holds the weight, and the car slows to a stop.

History

The Signature car design starts from the same history as the public record, the Russian ice slides and the Flip Flap Railway of 1895, and turns it into a sized design: a car, a lift chain, and a loop radius chosen so the track force supplies the centripetal requirement at the design speed. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a coaster car whose whole ride is calculated step by step before it is built.

Method

It sweeps out of the loop, the brakes catch it on the final run, and it rolls gently back into the station.

digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)

    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:

Anti-Lock Braking: Apply, Slip, Release, Reapply, Stop Rank 14 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature prototype anti-lock controller design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature anti-lock test car: a 1400 kilogram vehicle with a prototype controller, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.4.

This Signature design study proposes an anti-lock test car with a prototype controller: a 1400 kilogram vehicle, scaled from the public 1000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.4. The car weighs 13,734 newtons, held up by the same normal force. When the brakes apply, they push backward with 8400 newtons, so the net force is 8400 newtons backward and the car slows hard. If a wheel locks, the tyre slides and the grip falls to 4200 newtons backward, so the car slows half as fast and the wheel skids. The controller releases the brake, the net force is zero for an instant, and the wheel spins up again. Then the brakes reapply with 8400 newtons, and the car stops under control instead of skidding.

History

The Signature controller design starts from the same history as the public record, aircraft anti-lock systems of the 1950s and car systems of the 1970s, and turns it into a prototype: wheel-speed sensors, a controller, and a brake modulator on a test car. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network an anti-lock cycle whose release timing is set by measurement, not by guess.

Method

Build the 1400 kilogram test car and fit wheel-speed sensors.; Take the braking force at each step from the modulator.; Add the horizontal forces at each step to get the net force.; Note the slip step, where the grip falls and the net force halves.; Run the demo, which checks all five steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Apply — forces:
    • Braking force: 8400 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 13734 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 13734 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -8400 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -8400 N, Fy = 1.0287e-12 N; difference 2.08973e-12 N (tolerance 1.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Slip — forces:
    • Sliding friction: 4200 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 13734 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 13734 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -4200 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -4200 N, Fy = 5.14352e-13 N; difference 1.89031e-12 N (tolerance 1.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Release — forces:
    • Normal force: 13734 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 13734 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.68193e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.68193e-12 N (tolerance 1.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Reapply — forces:
    • Braking force: 8400 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 13734 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 13734 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -8400 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -8400 N, Fy = 1.0287e-12 N; difference 2.08973e-12 N (tolerance 1.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  5. Step 5: Stop — forces:
    • Normal force: 13734 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 13734 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.68193e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.68193e-12 N (tolerance 1.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: apply, slip, release, reapply, stop. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the release must be fast enough to restore rolling before the skid grows. A slow release lets the wheel stay locked and the car skid.

Physics basis

Step 1, apply: braking force 8400 newtons backward gives a net force of 8400 newtons backward, which slows the car hard. Step 2, slip: sliding friction 4200 newtons backward alone gives 4200 newtons backward, so the car slows half as fast and the wheel skids. Step 3, release: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the wheel spins up. Step 4, reapply: braking force 8400 newtons backward again gives 8400 newtons backward. Step 5, stop: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the car stands still.

Worked example

The Signature test car brakes hard on a wet track. It slows hard, then a wheel locks and the car starts to skid, so the prototype controller releases that brake for a fraction of a second, the wheel spins up, and the brake reapplies. The cycle repeats many times a second, the car stays steerable, and it stops in a shorter distance than it would have skidding.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature prototype anti-lock controller design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: the apply and reapply steps are the braking force alone, 8400 newtons backward, while the slip step is the sliding friction alone, 4200 newtons backward. The release and stop steps have no horizontal forces, so their net force is zero.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, a rolling tyre grips the road and a sliding tyre does not, so the moment a wheel locks the brakes stop working well. The prototype controller watches for that moment, lets go just long enough for the wheel to roll again, and then brakes again, repeating the cycle many times a second. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 5 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Apply': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -8400 N and Fy = 1.0287e-12 N, against a required net force of Fx = -8400 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 2.08973e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Slip': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4200 N and Fy = 5.14352e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -4200 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.89031e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Release': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.68193e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.68193e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Reapply': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -8400 N and Fy = 1.0287e-12 N, against a required net force of Fx = -8400 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 2.08973e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.4 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.68193e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.68193e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.4 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Apply: -8400 N; slip: -4200 N; release: 0 N; reapply: -8400 N; stop: 0 N (all horizontal, vertical forces cancel) (stated, not machine-checked)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Anti-Lock Braking: Apply, Slip, Release, Reapply, Stop'
steps = [('Apply', [('Braking force', 8400.0, 180), ('Normal force', 13734.0, 90), ('Gravity', 13734.0, 270)], [-8400.0, 0.0], 1.4), ('Slip', [('Sliding friction', 4200.0, 180), ('Normal force', 13734.0, 90), ('Gravity', 13734.0, 270)], [-4200.0, 0.0], 1.4), ('Release', [('Normal force', 13734.0, 90), ('Gravity', 13734.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.4), ('Reapply', [('Braking force', 8400.0, 180), ('Normal force', 13734.0, 90), ('Gravity', 13734.0, 270)], [-8400.0, 0.0], 1.4), ('Stop', [('Normal force', 13734.0, 90), ('Gravity', 13734.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.4)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Crane Lift: Hoist, Slew, Lower, Set Down Rank 15 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature tower crane lift plan design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature tower crane: a 2400 kilogram load design, scaled from the public 2000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.2.

This Signature design study proposes a tower crane lift plan for a heavier load: a 2400 kilogram load, scaled from the public 2000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.2. The load weighs 23,544 newtons. To hoist, the cable pulls up with 25,200 newtons, so the net force is 1656 newtons up and the load rises. To slew, the cable pulls with 23,544 newtons, exactly matching the weight, while a 600 newton sideways push moves the load across, so the net force is 600 newtons sideways. To lower, the cable pulls with only 21,600 newtons, so the net force is 1944 newtons down and the load descends. At the ground the cable goes slack, the ground pushes up with 23,544 newtons, and the load rests.

History

The Signature crane design starts from the same history as the public record, Roman treadwheel cranes and the tower cranes that build modern cities, and turns it into a sized lift plan: a cable, a load chart, and a tension for each step. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a crane lift whose every step is calculated before the hook is attached.

Method

Weigh the load and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Set the cable tension for the hoist step above the weight.; Set the cable tension equal to the weight for the slew step and add the sideways push.; Set the cable tension below the weight for the lower step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Hoist — forces:
    • Cable tension: 25200 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 23544 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 1656 N; actual net force Fx = -2.78191e-12 N, Fy = 1656 N; difference 2.78191e-12 N (tolerance 1.2 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Slew — forces:
    • Cable tension: 23544 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 23544 N at 270 degrees
    • Slew push: 600 N at 0 degrees
    required net force Fx = 600 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 600 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 2.84217e-12 N (tolerance 1.2 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Lower — forces:
    • Cable tension: 21600 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 23544 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -1944 N; actual net force Fx = -3.00234e-12 N, Fy = -1944 N; difference 3.00234e-12 N (tolerance 1.2 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Set down — forces:
    • Ground normal force: 23544 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 23544 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -2.88331e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 2.88331e-12 N (tolerance 1.2 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: hoist, slew, lower, set down. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical value: cable tension 25,200 newtons in the hoist step. The cable and the crane must be rated above the tension the lift demands.

Physics basis

Step 1, hoist: cable tension 25,200 newtons up against gravity 23,544 newtons down gives a net force of 1656 newtons up. Step 2, slew: cable tension 23,544 newtons up against gravity 23,544 newtons down gives zero vertical net force, and the slew push of 600 newtons sideways gives a net force of 600 newtons sideways. Step 3, lower: cable tension 21,600 newtons up against gravity 23,544 newtons down gives 1944 newtons down. Step 4, set down: ground push 23,544 newtons up against gravity 23,544 newtons down gives zero net force.

Worked example

The Signature tower crane picks up a 2400 kilogram steel beam from a truck. The cable tightens, the beam rises clear of the truck, and the crane slews it across the site while it hangs steady. The operator lowers it gently onto the frame, the cable goes slack as the beam's weight transfers to the structure, and the riggers unhook it.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature tower crane lift plan design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 2400 times 9.81 is 23,544, 25,200 minus 23,544 is 1656 for the hoist step, and 21,600 minus 23,544 is minus 1944 for the lower step. The slew step is an exact vertical balance, 23,544 against 23,544, with a 600 newton sideways net force.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the design does not lift by pulling as hard as possible; it lifts by pulling a little harder than the weight while the load rises, exactly as hard as the weight while it hangs, and a little less hard while it lowers. The difference between the cable pull and the weight is the net force, and the net force is what moves the load. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Hoist': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -2.78191e-12 N and Fy = 1656 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 1656 N; the difference is 2.78191e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.2 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Slew': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 600 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 600 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 2.84217e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.2 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Lower': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3.00234e-12 N and Fy = -1944 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -1944 N; the difference is 3.00234e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.2 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Set down': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -2.88331e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 2.88331e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.2 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Hoist: 25200 - 23544 = 1656 N up; slew: (600, 0) N; lower: 21600 - 23544 = -1944 N; set down: 0 N (stated, not machine-checked)

The difference between the cable pull and the weight is the net force, and the net force is what moves the load. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. Step 'Slew': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 600 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 600 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 2.84217e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.2 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Lower': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3.00234e-12 N and Fy = -1944 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -1944 N; the difference is 3.00234e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.2 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Crane Lift: Hoist, Slew, Lower, Set Down'
steps = [('Hoist', [('Cable tension', 25200.0, 90), ('Gravity', 23544.0, 270)], [0.0, 1656.0], 1.2), ('Slew', [('Cable tension', 23544.0, 90), ('Gravity', 23544.0, 270), ('Slew push', 600.0, 0)], [600.0, 0.0], 1.2), ('Lower', [('Cable tension', 21600.0, 90), ('Gravity', 23544.0, 270)], [0.0, -1944.0], 1.2), ('Set down', [('Ground normal force', 23544.0, 90), ('Gravity', 23544.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.2)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Diving Board: Jump, Board Spring, Flight, Water Entry Rank 16 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature diving board and diver design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature diving board: a 56 kilogram diver design, scaled from the public 70 kilogram case by a factor of 0.8.

This Signature design study proposes a diving board and diver combination for a lighter diver: a 56 kilogram diver, scaled from the public 70 kilogram case by a factor of 0.8. The diver weighs 549.36 newtons. The legs push up with 960 newtons, so the net force is 410.64 newtons up and the diver leaves the board rising. The board springs back with 1200 newtons up, so the net force is 650.64 newtons up and the diver is thrown higher. In flight gravity pulls down with 549.36 newtons plus 10.64 newtons of drag, so the net force is 560 newtons down and the diver arcs toward the water. On entry the water pushes up with 1600 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 1050.64 newtons up and the diver slows underwater.

History

The Signature board design starts from the same history as the public record, Olympic springboard diving from 1904 and the board as a spring that returns the diver's energy, and turns it into a sized design: a board stiffness chosen for a 56 kilogram diver and a pool depth chosen for the entry. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a board-and-diver pair whose whole jump is calculated step by step.

Method

Weigh the diver and compute the weight from mass times gravity.; Take the leg push and subtract the weight for the jump step.; Take the board force and subtract the weight for the board-spring step.; Add gravity and drag for the flight step and water drag for the entry step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Jump — forces:
    • Leg push: 960 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 549.36 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 410.64 N; actual net force Fx = -4.21327e-14 N, Fy = 410.64 N; difference 4.21327e-14 N (tolerance 0.4 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Board spring — forces:
    • Board force: 1200 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 549.36 N at 270 degrees

  4. Step 4: Water entry — forces:
    • Water drag: 1600 N at 90 degrees

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: jump, board spring, flight, water entry. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the board must return enough force to clear the pool edge, and the water must be deep enough to absorb the entry. Either shortfall breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, jump: leg push 960 newtons up against gravity 549.36 newtons down gives a net force of 410.64 newtons up. Step 2, board spring: board force 1200 newtons up against gravity 549.36 newtons down gives 650.64 newtons up. Step 3, flight: gravity 549.36 newtons plus air drag 10.64 newtons down gives 560 newtons down. Step 4, water entry: water drag 1600 newtons up against gravity 549.36 newtons down gives 1050.64 newtons up, which slows the diver underwater.

Worked example

A 56 kilogram diver bounces on the end of the Signature board, the board bending down and springing back. The legs push, the board throws the diver upward, and the diver rises, turns, and falls in a clean arc toward the pool. The water catches the entry with a rush of drag, the diver slows underwater, and surfaces to applause.

Science proof

In plain words, the board is a spring that the diver loads with a jump and that pays the energy back with interest, throwing the diver higher than a jump from the ground. Once the diver leaves the board, gravity takes over and the flight is a simple arc, until the water pushes back harder than gravity pulls and the diver slows. Spacecraft Rendezvous: Transfer Burn, Coast, Circularization, Docking Contact Rank 17 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature rendezvous profile design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature rendezvous design: a chaser and target pair in model units, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.1.

This Signature design study proposes a rendezvous profile for a chaser and target pair, in model units scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.1. The transfer burn pushes with 110 units forward against 88 units of gravity downward, so the net force is 110 units forward and 88 units down, lifting the chaser onto a higher path. On the coast, gravity alone pulls with 88 units down and the chaser drifts toward the target. The circularization burn pushes up with 88 units against gravity's 88 units down, so the net force is zero and the chaser matches the target's orbit. At docking contact, a 5.5 unit push meets a 5.5 unit thruster braking force, so the net force is zero and the two craft touch gently.

History

The Signature rendezvous design starts from the same history as the public record, Gemini 6 and 7 in December 1965 and Gemini 8's first docking in March 1966, and turns it into a profile design: burn sizes, a coast duration, and a docking approach speed, all stated in model units. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a rendezvous profile whose every step is calculated before it is flown.

Method

Take the model-unit thrust and gravity at each step.; Add thrust and gravity at each step to get the net force.; Check that the coast step has gravity alone as its net force.; Confirm the docking step has zero net force at contact.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

  1. Step 1: Transfer burn — forces:
    • Thrust: 110 model units at 0 degrees
    • Gravity: 88 model units at 270 degrees

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: transfer burn, coast, circularization, docking contact. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the transfer burn must place the chaser on a path that meets the target. A burn error grows during the coast and the rendezvous misses.

Physics basis

Step 1, transfer burn: thrust 110 model units forward against gravity 88 model units down gives a net force of 110 units forward and 88 units down. Step 2, coast: gravity 88 model units down alone gives a net force of 88 units down. Step 3, circularization: thrust 88 model units up against gravity 88 model units down gives zero net force. Step 4, docking contact: contact force 5.5 model units forward against thruster force 5.5 model units backward gives zero net force. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is the rendezvous profile itself, flown since Gemini in 1965.

Worked example

A Signature cargo craft chases the station around the Earth. It fires its engine for a transfer burn, coasts for hours while the gap closes, fires again to match the station's orbit, and then creeps in at centimetres per second until the docking mechanism catches with a soft clunk and the two craft become one.

The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Transfer: (110, -88) model units; coast: (0, -88); circularization: (0, 0); docking: (0, 0) (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

In plain words, a rendezvous is mostly waiting: two short burns set the chaser on the right path, and the long coast in between does the rest, because in orbit a small push now becomes a big change in position later. The final approach is slow on purpose, so the contact is a touch, not a crash. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Spacecraft Rendezvous: Transfer Burn, Coast, Circularization, Docking Contact'
steps = [('Transfer burn', [('Thrust', 110.0, 0), ('Gravity', 88.0, 270)], [110.0, -88.0], 0.55), ('Coast', [('Gravity', 88.0, 270)], [0.0, -88.0], 0.55), ('Circularization', [('Thrust', 88.0, 90), ('Gravity', 88.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.55), ('Docking contact', [('Contact force', 5.5, 0), ('Thruster', 5.5, 180)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.55)]
unit = 'model units'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance, not laboratory force readings; the established fact is stated in the record.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Gravitational Slingshot: Approach, Periapsis Bend, Departure Rank 18 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature slingshot flyby profile design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature slingshot design: a flyby profile in model units, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.3.

This Signature design study proposes a slingshot flyby profile in model units, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.3. On approach the planet pulls the craft down with 130 units, speeding it up as it falls toward the planet. At periapsis, the closest point, gravity pulls with 195 units down while the curved path supplies a centrifugal effect of 130 units up, so the net force is 65 units down and the craft's direction bends sharply around the planet. On departure gravity pulls with only 78 units down as the craft climbs away, and the craft leaves faster than it arrived, having borrowed speed from the planet's motion around the Sun.

History

The Signature flyby design starts from the same history as the public record, Kondratyuk in 1918, Zander in the 1920s, Mariner 10 at Venus in 1974, and Voyager's grand tour, and turns it into a profile design: an aim point, a periapsis distance, and a departure direction, all stated in model units. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a slingshot profile whose every step is calculated before it is flown.

Method

Take the model-unit gravity at each step.; Add gravity and the centrifugal effect at the periapsis step.; Check that the approach step has gravity alone as its net force.; Confirm the departure step has gravity alone, weaker with distance.; Run the demo, which checks all three steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Approach — forces:
    • Gravity: 130 model units at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = -130 model units; actual net force Fx = -2.38806e-14 model units, Fy = -130 model units; difference 2.38806e-14 model units (tolerance 0.65 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Periapsis bend — forces:
    • Gravity: 195 model units at 270 degrees
    • Centrifugal effect: 130 model units at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = -65 model units; actual net force Fx = -2.78607e-14 model units, Fy = -65 model units; difference 2.78607e-14 model units (tolerance 0.65 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Departure — forces:
    • Gravity: 78 model units at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = -78 model units; actual net force Fx = -1.43284e-14 model units, Fy = -78 model units; difference 1.43284e-14 model units (tolerance 0.65 model units); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: approach, periapsis bend, departure. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the aim at approach must place the craft at the planned periapsis distance. Too close and the craft enters the atmosphere; too far and the bend is too small.

Physics basis

Step 1, approach: gravity 130 model units down alone gives a net force of 130 units down, speeding the craft as it falls toward the planet. Step 2, periapsis bend: gravity 195 model units down against a centrifugal effect of 130 model units up gives 65 units down, which bends the path around the planet. Step 3, departure: gravity 78 model units down alone gives a net force of 78 units down as the craft climbs away, weaker with distance. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is the slingshot itself, flown by Voyager and Cassini.

Worked example

A Signature probe falls toward Jupiter, gaining speed as the giant planet pulls it in. It whips around the planet at closest approach, its path bent by gravity, and climbs away again, leaving faster than it arrived and aimed onward to Saturn, all without burning extra fuel. The same trick carried Cassini to Saturn on four flybys, and it is still the cheapest way to cross the Solar System.

Science proof

In plain words, a planet is a moving wall: a spacecraft that falls toward it and climbs away again leaves with some of the planet's speed added to its own, like a ball bouncing off a moving train. The bend at closest approach is what turns the fall into a new direction. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. Earthquake Base Isolation: Ground Shake, Isolator Flex, Damper Absorb, Settle Rank 19 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature base-isolation building design, at the public scale, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature base-isolation design: an isolated building in model units, at the same scale as the public case (factor 1.0).

This Signature design study proposes a base-isolated building with sized isolators and dampers, in model units at the same scale as the public case. When the ground shakes, it drives the building with 500 units sideways while the isolators push back with 400 units, so the net force is 100 units sideways and the building moves gently instead of snapping with the ground. As the isolators flex they push back with the full 500 units, so the net force is zero and the building rides out the shake. The dampers then absorb the motion, pushing back with 300 units against a 300 unit ground drive, so the net force is zero and the sway dies away. At settle the building's weight of 98,100 units down is held by the foundation's 98,100 units up, and the building stands still.

History

The Signature isolation design starts from the same history as the public record, the first isolated buildings of the 1970s in New Zealand and Japan, and turns it into a sized design: isolator bearings chosen for the building's weight and dampers chosen for the sway. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network an isolated building whose whole quake response is calculated step by step before it is built.

Method

Take the model-unit ground drive and isolator force at each step.; Size the isolators so their flex force matches the ground drive.; Check that the isolator-flex step has zero net force.; Confirm the settle step has the building weight matched by the foundation.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Ground shake — forces:
    • Ground drive: 500 model units at 0 degrees
    • Isolator force: 400 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 100 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 100 model units, Fy = 4.89859e-14 model units; difference 4.89859e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Isolator flex — forces:
    • Ground drive: 500 model units at 0 degrees
    • Isolator force: 500 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 6.12323e-14 model units; difference 6.12323e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Damper absorb — forces:
    • Ground drive: 300 model units at 0 degrees
    • Damper force: 300 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 3.67394e-14 model units; difference 3.67394e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Settle — forces:
    • Building weight: 98100 model units at 270 degrees
    • Foundation support: 98100 model units at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-11 model units, Fy = 0 model units; difference 1.20138e-11 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: ground shake, isolator flex, damper absorb, settle. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the isolators must flex far enough to take the ground motion without reaching their travel limit. A quake bigger than the design limit breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, ground shake: ground drive 500 model units sideways against isolator force 400 model units back gives a net force of 100 units sideways, so the building moves gently. Step 2, isolator flex: ground drive 500 model units against isolator force 500 model units gives zero net force. Step 3, damper absorb: ground drive 300 model units against damper force 300 model units gives zero net force, so the sway dies away. Step 4, settle: building weight 98,100 model units down against foundation support 98,100 model units up gives zero net force. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is base isolation itself, built into real hospitals.

Worked example

An earthquake shakes a Signature hospital that sits on rubber-and-steel isolators. The ground jerks sideways, the isolators flex, and the building above sways gently instead of whipping with the ground. The dampers soak up the motion, the swaying shrinks with each cycle, and when the shaking stops the building is still standing on its foundations, its equipment undamaged and its wards open.

Science proof

Scientific equation

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Earthquake Base Isolation: Ground Shake, Isolator Flex, Damper Absorb, Settle'
steps = [('Ground shake', [('Ground drive', 500.0, 0), ('Isolator force', 400.0, 180)], [100.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Isolator flex', [('Ground drive', 500.0, 0), ('Isolator force', 500.0, 180)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Damper absorb', [('Ground drive', 300.0, 0), ('Damper force', 300.0, 180)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Settle', [('Building weight', 98100.0, 270), ('Foundation support', 98100.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'model units'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance, not laboratory force readings; the established fact is stated in the record.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Sailboat Tack: Wind Drive, Turn, New Heading, Trim Rank 20 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature racing sailboat tack design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature racing sailboat: a tack design with a larger sail plan, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.2.

This Signature design study proposes a racing sailboat tack with a larger sail plan, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.2. With the wind pushing 960 newtons forward, the keel resisting with 600 newtons backward, and water drag of 120 newtons down the line, the net force is 360 newtons forward and 120 newtons sideways, and the boat drives ahead. In the turn the wind pushes 960 newtons sideways, the keel resists with 600 newtons, and the rudder pushes 240 newtons backward, so the net force is 240 newtons backward and 360 newtons sideways, swinging the bow through the wind. On the new heading the forces are the same as before the turn. Trimmed, the wind is 600 newtons, the keel 480, and the drag 120, so the net force is 120 newtons forward and 120 newtons sideways, and the boat holds its course.

History

The Signature boat design starts from the same history as the public record, the lateen sail of the medieval Mediterranean and the wing theory of the sail, and turns it into a sized design: a sail plan, a keel, and a rudder chosen for the boat's weight and speed. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a racing tack whose every step is calculated before it is sailed.

Method

Take the wind force, the keel resistance, and the water drag at each step.; Add the forces as vectors at each step to get the net force.; Check that the turn step's net force points the bow through the wind.; Confirm the trim step has a small steady forward net force.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Wind drive — forces:
    • Wind force: 960 N at 0 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 600 N at 180 degrees
    • Water drag: 120 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 360 N, Fy = -120 N; actual net force Fx = 360 N, Fy = -120 N; difference 7.10543e-14 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Turn — forces:
    • Wind force: 960 N at 90 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 600 N at 270 degrees
    • Rudder force: 240 N at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = -240 N, Fy = 360 N; actual net force Fx = -240 N, Fy = 360 N; difference 8.03887e-14 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: New heading — forces:
    • Wind force: 960 N at 0 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 600 N at 180 degrees
    • Water drag: 120 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 360 N, Fy = -120 N; actual net force Fx = 360 N, Fy = -120 N; difference 7.10543e-14 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Trim — forces:
    • Wind force: 600 N at 0 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 480 N at 180 degrees
    • Water drag: 120 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 120 N, Fy = -120 N; actual net force Fx = 120 N, Fy = -120 N; difference 6.35529e-14 N (tolerance 0.6 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: wind drive, turn, new heading, trim. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the boat must keep enough speed through the turn to steer. Too slow, and it stalls head-to-wind and the tack fails.

Physics basis

Step 1, wind drive: wind force 960 newtons forward against keel resistance 600 newtons backward and water drag 120 newtons sideways gives a net force of 360 newtons forward and 120 newtons sideways. Step 2, turn: wind force 960 newtons sideways against keel resistance 600 newtons and rudder force 240 newtons backward gives 240 newtons backward and 360 newtons sideways, swinging the bow through the wind. Step 3, new heading: the same forces as step 1 give 360 newtons forward and 120 newtons sideways. Step 4, trim: wind force 600 newtons against keel resistance 480 newtons and drag 120 newtons gives 120 newtons forward and 120 newtons sideways.

Worked example

The Signature racing boat beats upwind toward a mark. It sails close-hauled with the wind on one side, the crew calls the tack, the helm goes over, and the bow swings through the wind while the sails flap and fill on the other side. The crew trims the sheets, the boat settles on its new heading, and the zigzag continues toward the mark.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature racing sailboat tack design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 960 minus 600 is 360 forward and the drag is 120 sideways for the wind-drive step, and 960 minus 600 is 360 sideways with the rudder's 240 backward for the turn step. The trim step is 600 minus 480, which is 120 forward.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the design does not sail by being pushed from behind; its sail works like a wing, pulling the boat forward even when the wind is in front of it, while the keel stops the boat from sliding sideways. A tack is the boat turning through the wind so the wing can pull from the other side. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Wind drive': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 360 N and Fy = -120 N, against a required net force of Fx = 360 N and Fy = -120 N; the difference is 7.10543e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Turn': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -240 N and Fy = 360 N, against a required net force of Fx = -240 N and Fy = 360 N; the difference is 8.03887e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'New heading': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 360 N and Fy = -120 N, against a required net force of Fx = 360 N and Fy = -120 N; the difference is 7.10543e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.6 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Trim': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 120 N and Fy = -120 N, against a required net force of Fx = 120 N and Fy = -120 N; the difference is 6.35529e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.6 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

unit = 'N'

    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)

    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
