Gymnast Vault: Run, Springboard, Flight, Landing Rank 21 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature gymnast vault design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature vault design: a 54 kilogram gymnast and springboard pair, scaled from the public 60 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9.

This Signature design study proposes a vault combination for a lighter gymnast: a 54 kilogram gymnast, scaled from the public 60 kilogram case by a factor of 0.9. The gymnast weighs 529.74 newtons. On the run the legs drive forward with 630 newtons against 90 newtons of friction, so the net force is 540 newtons forward and the gymnast builds speed. On the springboard the board pushes up with 1350 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 820.26 newtons up and the gymnast is launched. In flight gravity alone pulls down with 529.74 newtons, so the net force is 529.74 newtons down and the gymnast arcs over the vault. On landing the mat pushes up with 1080 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 550.26 newtons up and the gymnast is brought to a stop.

History

The Signature vault design starts from the same history as the public record, vaulting at the first modern Olympics in 1896 and the springboard that changed it into a launch, and turns it into a sized design: a runway, a board stiffness, and a mat chosen for a 54 kilogram gymnast. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a vault whose whole sequence is calculated step by step before it is performed.

Method

Weigh the gymnast and compute the weight from mass times gravity.; Take the leg drive and subtract friction for the run step.; Take the board force and subtract the weight for the springboard step.; Take gravity alone for the flight step and the mat force for the landing step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Run — forces:
    • Leg drive: 630 N at 0 degrees
    • Friction: 90 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 529.74 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 529.74 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 540 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 540 N, Fy = 1.10218e-14 N; difference 1.1422e-13 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Springboard — forces:
    • Board force: 1350 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 529.74 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 820.26 N; actual net force Fx = -1.4648e-14 N, Fy = 820.26 N; difference 1.4648e-14 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Flight — forces:
    • Gravity: 529.74 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -529.74 N; actual net force Fx = -9.73117e-14 N, Fy = -529.74 N; difference 9.73117e-14 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Landing — forces:
    • Mat force: 1080 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 529.74 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 550.26 N; actual net force Fx = -3.11807e-14 N, Fy = 550.26 N; difference 3.11807e-14 N (tolerance 0.45 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: run, springboard, flight, landing. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical condition: the run must build enough speed for the board to launch the gymnast over the vault. Too slow, and the gymnast never clears it.

Physics basis

Step 1, run: leg drive 630 newtons forward against friction 90 newtons backward gives a net force of 540 newtons forward. Step 2, springboard: board force 1350 newtons up against gravity 529.74 newtons down gives 820.26 newtons up. Step 3, flight: gravity 529.74 newtons down alone gives a net force of 529.74 newtons down. Step 4, landing: mat force 1080 newtons up against gravity 529.74 newtons down gives 550.26 newtons up, which stops the gymnast.

Worked example

A 54 kilogram gymnast sprints down the Signature runway, hits the springboard with both feet, and is thrown upward, twisting over the vault in a clean arc. The hands touch the vault, the body straightens, and the gymnast comes down feet first on the mat, the mat giving under the landing and bringing the gymnast to a stop, arms raised.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature gymnast vault design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 54 times 9.81 is 529.74, 630 minus 90 is 540 for the run step, and 1350 minus 529.74 is 820.26 for the springboard step. The flight step is gravity alone at 529.74 down, and the landing step is 1080 minus 529.74, which is 550.26.

Science proof

In plain words, the design is a trade: speed from the run becomes height from the board, and height becomes time in the air to turn and twist. The board is a spring that pays back the gymnast's speed as an upward push, and the mat is a cushion that spreads the landing over time so the stop is gentle. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Step 'Flight': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -9.73117e-14 N and Fy = -529.74 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -529.74 N; the difference is 9.73117e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.45 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Gymnast Vault: Run, Springboard, Flight, Landing'
steps = [('Run', [('Leg drive', 630.0, 0), ('Friction', 90.0, 180), ('Normal force', 529.74, 90), ('Gravity', 529.74, 270)], [540.0, 0.0], 0.45), ('Springboard', [('Board force', 1350.0, 90), ('Gravity', 529.74, 270)], [0.0, 820.26], 0.45), ('Flight', [('Gravity', 529.74, 270)], [0.0, -529.74], 0.45), ('Landing', [('Mat force', 1080.0, 90), ('Gravity', 529.74, 270)], [0.0, 550.26], 0.45)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Particle Accelerator: Injection, Radio-Frequency Acceleration, Magnetic Bending, Extraction Rank 22 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature accelerator cycle design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature accelerator design: a beam cycle in model units, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.4.

This Signature design study proposes an accelerator cycle design in model units, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.4. Injection kicks the beam with 70 units forward, so the net force is 70 units forward and the particles enter the ring. The radio-frequency cavities kick with 56 units forward against 7 units of drag, so the net force is 49 units forward and the beam gains energy on every lap. The bending magnets pull with 140 units down while the cavities add 14 units forward, so the net force is 14 units forward and 140 units down, bending the beam around the ring. At extraction a kicker pushes up with 112 units against the bending magnets' 112 units down, so the net force is zero and the beam leaves the ring cleanly toward its target experiment.

History

The Signature accelerator design starts from the same history as the public record, Lawrence's first cyclotron in 1930 and the synchrotrons that followed, and turns it into a cycle design: an injector, cavity kicks, bending magnets, and a kicker, all stated in model units. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network an accelerator cycle whose every step is calculated before it is built.

Method

Take the model-unit injection kick for the first step.; Add the radio-frequency kick and subtract drag for the acceleration step.; Add the magnetic force and the cavity kick for the bending step.; Balance the kicker against the magnets for the extraction step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Injection — forces:
    • Injection kick: 70 model units at 0 degrees
    required net force Fx = 70 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 70 model units, Fy = 0 model units; difference 0 model units (tolerance 0.7 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Radio-frequency acceleration — forces:
    • Radio-frequency kick: 56 model units at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 7 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 49 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 49 model units, Fy = 8.57253e-16 model units; difference 8.57253e-16 model units (tolerance 0.7 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Magnetic bending — forces:
    • Magnetic force: 140 model units at 270 degrees
    • Radio-frequency kick: 14 model units at 0 degrees
    required net force Fx = 14 model units, Fy = -140 model units; actual net force Fx = 14 model units, Fy = -140 model units; difference 2.4869e-14 model units (tolerance 0.7 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Extraction — forces:
    • Kicker force: 112 model units at 90 degrees

    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = -1.3716e-14 model units, Fy = 0 model units; difference 1.3716e-14 model units (tolerance 0.7 model units); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: injection, radio-frequency acceleration, magnetic bending, extraction. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the bending magnets must supply the centripetal force for the beam's energy at every lap. A magnet failure lets the beam hit the wall.

Physics basis

Step 1, injection: injection kick 70 model units forward alone gives a net force of 70 units forward. Step 2, radio-frequency acceleration: radio-frequency kick 56 model units forward against drag 7 model units backward gives 49 units forward. Step 3, magnetic bending: magnetic force 140 model units down plus radio-frequency kick 14 model units forward gives a net force of 14 units forward and 140 units down, which bends the beam around the ring. Step 4, extraction: kicker force 112 model units up against magnetic force 112 model units down gives zero net force. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is the accelerator cycle itself, run daily in machines from hospitals to the Large Hadron Collider.

Worked example

In the Signature design, protons are injected into the ring, kicked forward by radio-frequency cavities on every lap, and bent around the ring by magnets thousands of times a second. When they reach the treatment energy, a kicker magnet nudges them out of the ring and down a beamline to the patient, where they stop in the tumour and deliver their dose.

Overloaded Crane Lift: Hoist, Overload, Cable Yield, Collapse Rank 23 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature destructive overload-test design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature destructive-test design: a crane lift driven past its rating on purpose, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.1, to demonstrate the collapse.

This Signature design study proposes a destructive test that drives a crane lift past its rating on purpose, to demonstrate the collapse safely in a test yard: a 2200 kilogram load, scaled from the public 2000 kilogram case by a factor of 1.1. The load weighs 21,582 newtons. The hoist step is sound: the cable pulls up with 23,100 newtons, so the net force is 1518 newtons up and the load rises. Then extra load is added until the weight is 27,500 newtons, while the cable still pulls with only 23,100 newtons, so the net force is 4400 newtons down against a required net force of zero: the load cannot be held and starts to fall. The cable yields under the overload, its pull dropping to 16,500 newtons, so the net force is 11,000 newtons down. Finally the cable parts, the pull is zero, and the net force is the full 27,500 newtons down as the load falls into the test pit.

History

The Signature test design starts from the same history as the public record, crane safety ratings and the accident reports that created them, and turns it into a planned destructive test: a sacrificial cable, a test pit, and a load added step by step past the rating. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a demonstrated overload collapse, measured in a yard where nothing else can be hurt.

Method

Weigh the load and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Check the hoist step, where the cable pull exceeds the weight.; Add the extra load and compare the new weight with the cable pull.; Note the yield step, where the cable pull falls as the cable stretches past its limit.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and shows the sequence failing..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Hoist — forces:
    • Cable tension: 23100 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 21582 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 1518 N; actual net force Fx = -2.55008e-12 N, Fy = 1518 N; difference 2.55008e-12 N (tolerance 1.1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Overload — forces:
    • Cable tension: 23100 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 27500 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -3.6372e-12 N, Fy = -4400 N; difference 4400 N (tolerance 1.1 N); step verdict FAILS.
  3. Step 3: Cable yield — forces:
    • Cable tension: 16500 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 27500 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -4.04133e-12 N, Fy = -11000 N; difference 11000 N (tolerance 1.1 N); step verdict FAILS.
  4. Step 4: Collapse — forces:
    • Gravity: 27500 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -5.05167e-12 N, Fy = -27500 N; difference 27500 N (tolerance 1.1 N); step verdict FAILS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: hoist, overload, cable yield, collapse. The sequence collapses: the overload step fails because the weight exceeds the cable pull, and the later steps fail worse.

Collapse verdict

collapses — Critical value: safe working load 21,582 newtons against a cable pull of 23,100 newtons. Past the rating the cable cannot hold, and the sequence collapses.

Physics basis

Step 1, hoist: cable tension 23,100 newtons up against gravity 21,582 newtons down gives a net force of 1518 newtons up, which holds. Step 2, overload: cable tension 23,100 newtons up against gravity 27,500 newtons down gives 4400 newtons down against a required net force of zero, so the step fails and the load starts to fall. Step 3, cable yield: cable tension 16,500 newtons up against gravity 27,500 newtons down gives 11,000 newtons down, so the step fails. Step 4, collapse: gravity 27,500 newtons down alone gives 27,500 newtons down, so the load falls.

Worked example

In the Signature test yard a crane lifts a 2200 kilogram beam, and a second load is hooked on without checking the chart, taking the weight to 27,500 newtons. The cable strains, the load starts to sink instead of holding, and the cable yields, stretching and weakening until it parts. The load falls into the test pit. The test report records the same three numbers this demo records: the rated load, the actual load, and the cable that could not hold it.

Evidence

design-study — Design study: an original Signature destructive overload-test design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

Verification path

Check by hand: 2200 times 9.81 is 21,582, and 23,100 minus 21,582 is 1518 for the hoist step. The overload step is 23,100 minus 27,500, which is minus 4400, and the yield step is 16,500 minus 27,500, which is minus 11,000, so both fail their required zero net force by far.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, a cable can only pull as hard as its steel allows, and a load heavier than that pull will fall no matter what the operator wants. The overload step is the moment the numbers stop adding up: the weight is bigger than the pull, so the net force points down and the load accelerates downward. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order, and the last three steps fail. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: at least one step fails its tolerance check, so the whole sequence fails.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Hoist': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -2.55008e-12 N and Fy = 1518 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 1518 N; the difference is 2.55008e-12 N against a tolerance of 1.1 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Overload': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3.6372e-12 N and Fy = -4400 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 4400 N against a tolerance of 1.1 N, so the step FAILS. Step 'Cable yield': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4.04133e-12 N and Fy = -11000 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 11000 N against a tolerance of 1.1 N, so the step FAILS. Step 'Collapse': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -5.05167e-12 N and Fy = -27500 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 27500 N against a tolerance of 1.1 N, so the step FAILS. The whole sequence therefore fails. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Overloaded Crane Lift: Hoist, Overload, Cable Yield, Collapse'
steps = [('Hoist', [('Cable tension', 23100.0, 90), ('Gravity', 21582.0, 270)], [0.0, 1518.0], 1.1), ('Overload', [('Cable tension', 23100.0, 90), ('Gravity', 27500.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.1), ('Cable yield', [('Cable tension', 16500.0, 90), ('Gravity', 27500.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.1), ('Collapse', [('Gravity', 27500.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.1)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Spring Over-Compression: Compress, Release, Over-Compress, Yield Rank 24 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: an original Signature destructive spring-yield test design, scaled from the public case, with a demo-verified force sequence.

A Signature destructive-test design: a spring forced past its elastic limit on purpose, scaled from the public case by a factor of 1.3, to demonstrate the yield.

This Signature design study proposes a destructive test that forces a spring past its elastic limit on purpose, to demonstrate the yield: a 6.5 kilogram mass on a spring of stiffness 100 newtons per metre, scaled from the public 5 kilogram case by a factor of 1.3. The mass weighs 63.77 newtons, and the spring is rated to an elastic limit of 104 newtons of spring force. Compressed inside the range, the hand pushes down with 39 newtons, the spring pushes up with 102.77 newtons, and gravity pulls down with 63.77 newtons, so the net force is zero. Released, the spring's 102.77 newtons up beats gravity's 63.77 newtons down, so the net force is 39 newtons up. Forced past the limit, the hand pushes down with 130 newtons while the spring can only supply its limit of 104 newtons up, so the net force is 89.77 newtons down against a required zero: the spring yields. After yielding the spring only supplies 52 newtons up against the weight, so the net force is 11.77 newtons down and the mass never returns to its old balance.

History

The Signature test design starts from the same history as the public record, Hooke's 1678 spring law and the elastic limit, and turns it into a planned destructive test: a bench press that loads a spring step by step past its rating. The design study was drawn up in November 2026 to give the Signature network a demonstrated spring yield, measured on a bench where the failed spring can be kept as evidence.

Method

Weigh the mass and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Take the spring stiffness and the elastic limit of 104 newtons of spring force.; Add hand, spring, and gravity at the compress step and check the sum is zero.; Push past the limit and compare the hand force with the spring's maximum.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and shows the sequence failing..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Compress — forces:
    • Hand push: 39 N at 270 degrees
    • Spring force: 102.77 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 63.77 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.25857e-14 N, Fy = -7.10543e-15 N; difference 1.44529e-14 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Release — forces:
    • Spring force: 102.77 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 63.77 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 39 N; actual net force Fx = -5.42151e-15 N, Fy = 39 N; difference 8.93755e-15 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Over-compress — forces:
    • Hand push: 130 N at 270 degrees
    • Spring force: 104 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 63.77 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -2.92268e-14 N, Fy = -89.77 N; difference 89.77 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict FAILS.
  4. Step 4: Yield — forces:
    • Spring force: 52 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 63.77 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -8.53028e-15 N, Fy = -11.77 N; difference 11.77 N (tolerance 0.65 N); step verdict FAILS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: compress, release, over-compress, yield. The sequence collapses: the over-compress step fails because the hand exceeds the spring's elastic limit, and the yield step fails after it.

Collapse verdict

collapses — Critical value: elastic limit 104 newtons of spring force. Past it the spring yields permanently, and the sequence collapses.

Physics basis

Step 1, compress: spring force 102.77 newtons up against hand push 39 newtons down and gravity 63.77 newtons down gives zero net force. Step 2, release: spring force 102.77 newtons up against gravity 63.77 newtons down gives 39 newtons up. Step 3, over-compress: spring force 104 newtons up, its elastic limit, against hand push 130 newtons down and gravity 63.77 newtons down gives 89.77 newtons down against a required net force of zero, so the step fails and the spring yields. Step 4, yield: spring force 52 newtons up against gravity 63.77 newtons down gives 11.77 newtons down, so the step fails and the mass never returns to its old balance.

Worked example

A student compresses the Signature spring with a 6.5 kilogram mass, holding it with a 39 newton push, and lets go: the mass bounces happily. Then the student leans on it with 130 newtons, past the spring's 104 newton limit. The spring gives with a soft click and stays bent. Afterwards the mass hangs lower than before, because the yielded spring only pushes with 52 newtons where it used to push with 63.77.

Verification path

Check by hand: 6.5 times 9.81 is 63.77, and 102.77 minus 63.77 minus 39 is zero for the compress step, while 102.77 minus 63.77 is 39 for the release step. The over-compress step is 104 minus 63.77 minus 130, which is minus 89.77, failing its required zero.

After that the spring is a different, weaker spring, and the old balance point is gone for good. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order, and the last two steps fail. import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Spring Over-Compression: Compress, Release, Over-Compress, Yield'
steps = [('Compress', [('Hand push', 39.0, 270), ('Spring force', 102.77, 90), ('Gravity', 63.77, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.65), ('Release', [('Spring force', 102.77, 90), ('Gravity', 63.77, 270)], [0.0, 39.0], 0.65), ('Over-compress', [('Hand push', 130.0, 270), ('Spring force', 104.0, 90), ('Gravity', 63.77, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.65), ('Yield', [('Spring force', 52.0, 90), ('Gravity', 63.77, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.65)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):

    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():

    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])

        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()

results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature sequence design, not an established public fact.")
